The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\sin x}{x} \) can be evaluated using multiple methods:

1. **Squeeze Theorem**: For small positive angles, it's established that \( \cos x \leq \frac{\sin x}{x} \leq 1 \). As \( x \to 0 \), both bounds approach 1, so by the squeeze theorem, the limit is 1.

2. **L'Hospital’s Rule**: Since both numerator and denominator approach 0 (indeterminate form 0/0), we differentiate to get \( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\cos x}{1} = \cos(0) = 1 \).

3. **Taylor Series Expansion**: The Taylor series of \( \sin x \) around 0 is \( x - \frac{x^3}{6} + \cdots \). Dividing by \( x \), the leading term gives 1 as \( x \to 0 \).

All methods consistently show that the limit is 1.

**Final Answer:**
The limit is 1.

The limit of sqrt(x² + 2x) - x as x approaches infinity can be found by rationalizing and simplifying:

lim_{x → ∞} [sqrt(x² + 2x) - x] = 1

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Original Expression:** 
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \left( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \right)
   \]

2. **Rationalize the Expression:**
   Multiply numerator and denominator by the conjugate:
   \[
   \frac{(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x)(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x)}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
   \]
   
3. **Simplify Numerator:**
   Using the difference of squares:
   \[
   (\sqrt{x^2 + 2x})^2 - x^2 = (x^2 + 2x) - x^2 = 2x
   \]
   
4. **Resulting Expression:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
   \]

5. **Factor Out \(x\) from Denominator’s Square Root:**
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = |x|\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} 
   \]
   Since \(x\) approaches infinity (positive), this simplifies to:
   \[
   x\left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)
   \]

6. **Simplify Denominator:**
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x = x + x = 2x
   \]
   
7. **Final Simplification:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{2x}{2x} = 1
   \]

**Answer:**  
The limit is \(\boxed{1}\).

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Factor the Numerator:**
   The numerator \( x^2 - 4 \) is a difference of squares and can be factored as:
   \[
   x^2 - 4 = (x - 2)(x + 2)
   \]

2. **Simplify the Expression:**
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression:
   \[
   \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2} = \frac{(x - 2)(x + 2)}{x - 2}
   \]
   
   Since \( x \neq 2 \) (because we're taking the limit as \( x \) approaches 2), we can cancel out the \( x - 2 \) terms:
   \[
   \frac{(x - 2)(x + 2)}{x - 2} = x + 2
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Simplified Expression:**
   Now, take the limit as \( x \) approaches 2:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 2} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4
   \]

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\tan x}{x}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Problem:**
   We need to evaluate the limit of \( \frac{\tan x}{x} \) as \( x \) approaches 0.

2. **Direct Substitution:**
   Directly substituting \( x = 0 \) results in:
   \[
   \frac{\tan(0)}{0} = \frac{0}{0}
   \]
   This is an indeterminate form, so we need another method to evaluate the limit.

3. **Use L'Hospital's Rule:**
   Since both the numerator and denominator approach 0 as \( x \) approaches 0, we can apply **L'Hospital's Rule**, which states that:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
   \]
   provided the limit on the right exists.

4. **Differentiate Numerator and Denominator:**
   - The derivative of \( \tan x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \sec^2 x \).
   - The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1.

5. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\tan x}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sec^2 x}{1}
   \]

6. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   \[
   \sec^2(0) = (1)^2 = 1
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

L’Hospital’s Rule states that if lim f(x)/g(x) as x approaches a gives 0/0 or ±∞/±∞, then the limit can be found by taking the derivatives of the numerator and denominator separately, provided the new limit exists.

So applying L’Hospital’s Rule here:

Next, find the derivative of the denominator: d/dx [x] = 1.

Now, substituting these into L’Hospital’s formula:

   - The numerator is \( x^2 + x + 1 \).
   - The denominator is \( x^2 - x + 1 \).

To find the limit \(\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1}\), we can use L'Hospital's Rule since both the numerator and denominator approach zero as \(x\) approaches 1.

First, we check the form of the limit:
- The numerator at \(x = 1\) is \((2^{1/3} - 1)\), which is approximately 0.26.
- The denominator at \(x = 1\) is 0.

Thus, it is a \(\frac{0}{0}\) form, and we can apply L'Hospital's Rule:
\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1} = \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{\frac{d}{dx}[(1 + x^3)^{1/3}]}{\frac{d}{dx}(x - 1)}
\]

We compute the derivative of the numerator:
- Let \(f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3}\).
- The derivative is \(f'(x) = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}}\).

The derivative of the denominator is 1.

Thus, the limit becomes:
\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{\frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}}}{1}
\]

Evaluating this at \(x = 1\):
- The numerator is \(1^2 = 1\).
- The denominator is \((1 + 1^3)^{2/3} = 2^{2/3}\).

Therefore, the limit is:
\[
\frac{1}{2^{2/3}} = 2^{-2/3}
\]

The final answer is:
\[
\boxed{2^{-2/3}}
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll use basic differentiation rules.

1. **Differentiate each term separately**:
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \) is found using the power rule: 
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x
     \]
   - The derivative of \( -5x \) is:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \) is:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives**:
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

I recall that the power rule is really useful for this kind of problem. The power rule states that if you have a function f(x) = x^n, then the derivative f'(x) = n*x^(n-1). Okay, so maybe I can rewrite 1/x in a form that fits into this rule.

The derivative of f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} can be found using either the chain rule or the quotient rule. Both methods lead to the same result: f'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}.

**Step-by-Step Explanation Using Chain Rule:**

1. **Rewrite the Function:** Start by expressing f(x) as (x² + 1)⁻¹.
   
2. **Apply the Chain Rule:** Let u = x² + 1, so f(u) = u⁻¹.
   - Derivative of the outer function with respect to u: d/du [u⁻¹] = -u⁻².
   - Derivative of the inner function with respect to x: du/dx = 2x.

3. **Multiply the Derivatives:** f’(x) = (-u⁻²) * (2x) = -2x/(x² + 1)².

**Verification Using Quotient Rule:**

Alternatively, using the quotient rule with numerator u = 1 and denominator v = x² + 1:
- du/dx = 0.
- dv/dx = 2x.

Apply the quotient rule formula:
f’(x) = [v*du/dx - u*dv/dx]/v²
= [(x² + 1)(0) - (1)(2x)] / (x² + 1)²
= (-2x)/(x² + 1)².

Both methods confirm that the derivative is:
f’(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}.

   - Outer function: \( e^u \)
   - Inner function: \( u = x^2 \)

2. **Differentiate each function**:
   - Derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \): \( \frac{d}{du} e^u = e^u \)
   - Derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \): \( \frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2x \)

3. **Apply the Chain Rule**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} e^{x^2} = \frac{d}{du} e^u \cdot \frac{d}{dx} x^2 = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer**:  
\boxed{2xe^{x^2}}

- Let \( u = \sin x \)
- Then, \( f(u) = \ln(u) \)

**Step 2: Differentiate Using the Chain Rule**
\[
f'(x) = \frac{d}{du} \ln(u) \times \frac{d}{dx} \sin x
\]

**Step 3: Compute Each Derivative**
- \( \frac{d}{du} \ln(u) = \frac{1}{u} \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx} \sin x = \cos x \)

**Step 4: Substitute Back and Simplify**
\[

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), I'll start by recognizing that this function is a quotient of two exponential functions. Since both the numerator and denominator are differentiable, I should use the Quotient Rule.

The Quotient Rule states that if you have \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), then the derivative \( f'(x) \) is given by:

\[ f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} \]

First, I'll identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):

- \( u(x) = e^x \)
- \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \)

Next, I need to find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):

- The derivative of \( u(x) = e^x \) is \( u'(x) = e^x \).
- The derivative of \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \) is \( v'(x) = e^x \).

Now, I'll substitute these into the Quotient Rule formula:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Let's simplify the numerator step by step:

First term: \( e^x(1 + e^x) = e^x + (e^x)^2 \)

Second term: \( -e^x(e^x) = - (e^x)^2 \)

When we combine these terms:

\[
e^x + (e^x)^2 - (e^x)^2 = e^x
\]

So, the numerator simplifies to \( e^x \).

Therefore, the derivative becomes:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]
</think>

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we'll use the **Quotient Rule** from calculus. The Quotient Rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative \( f'(x) \) is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

- Let \( u(x) = e^x \)
- Let \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \)

**Step 2: Compute the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

- \( u'(x) = e^x \) (since the derivative of \( e^x \) is itself)
- \( v'(x) = e^x \) (since the derivative of \( 1 + e^x \) is \( e^x \))

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
= \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Numerator**

Simplify \( e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x) \):

\[
e^x + (e^x)^2 - (e^x)^2 = e^x
\]

So, the numerator simplifies to \( e^x \).

**Step 5: Write the Final Derivative**

\[
f'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

This is the derivative of the given function.

Step-by-step explanation:

To find f''(x), start with f'(x) = 2x e^{x²}.

Differentiate f'(x) using the product rule:
- Let u = 2x ⇒ u' = 2
- Let v = e^{x²} ⇒ v' = 2x e^{x²}

Now for v(x) = 1 + x². The derivative of that, v’, would be the derivative of 1 (which is 0) plus the derivative of x squared. I think the derivative of x² is 2x, so v’ should be 2x.

Okay, now plugging these into the quotient rule formula: f’(x) = [u’v - uv’] / v². Substituting in the values we just found:

f’(x) = [1*(1 + x²) - x*(2x)] / (1 + x²)².

Let me compute the numerator step by step. First, 1 times (1 + x²) is just 1 + x². Then, subtracting x times 2x, which is 2x². So:

Numerator = (1 + x²) - 2x² = 1 + x² - 2x².

Combining like terms: 1 - x².

So now the derivative simplifies to f’(x) = (1 - x²) / (1 + x²) squared, which is (1 + x²)^2 in the denominator.

Wait, let me double-check that. The original function was x over (1 + x²), so when I apply the quotient rule correctly, it's [1*(1 + x²) - x*2x] all over (1 + x²)^2. That simplifies to [1 + x² - 2x²], which is indeed 1 - x² in the numerator.

So yeah, f’(x) = (1 - x²)/(1 + x²)^2. I think that's it. Let me see if there's any further simplification needed or if this can be factored more. Hmm, 1 - x² is a difference of squares; it factors into (1 - x)(1 + x). The denominator is already squared, so maybe we can write the derivative as [(1 - x)(1 + x)] / (1 + x²)^2.

But I don't think that's necessary unless the question specifically asks for factoring. Since they just asked for the derivative, this should be sufficient. Let me quickly verify by plugging in a value to see if it makes sense. For example, at x = 0: f(0) is 0/(1 + 0) which is 0. The derivative would be (1 - 0)/(1)^2 = 1. That means the slope at x=0 is positive 1, which seems reasonable since as x increases from 0, f(x) goes from 0 upwards.

Another test: at x approaching infinity, what happens to f’(x)? The denominator grows faster than the numerator because it's squared in the denominator and linear in the numerator. So the derivative tends towards 0, meaning the function flattens out as x approaches infinity, which also makes sense because for large x, f(x) ≈ x/x² = 1/x, which approaches 0.

Okay, I feel confident that my application of the quotient rule is correct and that the simplification steps are accurate.
</think>

The derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) is found using the quotient rule. 

Let \( u = x \) with \( u' = 1 \), and \( v = 1 + x^2 \) with \( v' = 2x \). Applying the quotient rule:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

Simplifying the numerator:

\[
(1 + x^2) - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

Thus, the derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

Final Answer: {"Numeric answer": "\frac{1 - x^{2}}{(1 + x^{2})^{2}}"}

To find the limit \(\displaystyle \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}\), we can simplify the expression by factoring the numerator and canceling common terms.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Factor the Numerator:**
   
   The numerator \(x^2 - 4\) is a difference of squares, which can be factored as:
   \[
   x^2 - 4 = (x - 2)(x + 2)
   \]

2. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression and cancel the common factor of \(x - 2\) in the numerator and denominator:
   \[
   \frac{(x - 2)(x + 2)}{x - 2} = x + 2 \quad \text{(for } x \neq 2\text{)}
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   
   Now, we can directly substitute \(x = 2\) into the simplified expression:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 2} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4
   \]

The limit is found by recognizing that as \( x \) approaches infinity, \( \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x \) approaches Euler's number \( e \).

Step-by-step explanation:

1. Let \( y = \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x \).
2. Take the natural logarithm: \( \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln\left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right) \).
3. Use the Taylor series expansion for \( \ln(1 + t) \approx t - \frac{t^2}{2} + \ldots \), where \( t = \frac{1}{x} \).
4. Substitute to get \( \ln(y) \approx x\left(\frac{1}{x} - \frac{1}{2x^2}\right) = 1 - \frac{1}{2x} \).
5. As \( x \to \infty \), \( \ln(y) \to 1 \).
6. Exponentiate both sides: \( y \to e^1 = e \).

Final Answer:
The limit is \( e \).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will use basic differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   
   - For \( 3x^2 \):
     - Apply the power rule: \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = nx^{n-1} \).
     - The derivative is \( 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x \).

   - For \( -5x \):
     - The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1.
     - So, the derivative is \( -5 \times 1 = -5 \).

The derivative of \( \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is a fundamental result in calculus and can be derived using either first principles or known properties of inverse functions.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

   - Start with the definition of derivative:
     \[
     f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\ln(x + h) - \ln x}{h}
     \]
   - Use logarithm properties to combine terms:
     \[

     \]
   - Simplify further and recognize the limit as \( h/x \) approaches 0, leading to the result.

   - Recognize that \( f(x) = e^x \) is the inverse of \( \ln x \).

4. **Apply Derivative Rule:**
   - The standard result for the derivative of \( \ln x \) is known:
     \[
     f'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
     \]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we'll use the **Product Rule**. The Product Rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x) \).

Let's break down our function:

- **First function (\( u(x) \))**: \( \cos x \)
  - Derivative of \( \cos x \) is \( -\sin x \). So, \( u'(x) = -\sin x \).
  
- **Second function (\( v(x) \))**: \( \ln x \)
  - Derivative of \( \ln x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \). So, \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

Now, applying the Product Rule:

\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
f'(x) = (-\sin x) \cdot (\ln x) + (\cos x) \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right )
\]
\[
f'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

So, the derivative of \( f(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \) is:

\[
f'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

\[
\ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
\]
Using the property of logarithms, this simplifies to:
\[
\ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
\]

Next, I'll differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \). Applying the chain rule on the left side and the product rule on the right side:

For the left side:
\[
\frac{d}{dx}[\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot y'
\]
For the right side, differentiating \( x \cdot \ln(x) \):
\[
\frac{d}{dx}[x \cdot \ln(x)] = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1
\]

Putting it all together:
\[
\frac{y'}{y} = \ln(x) + 1
\]
Solving for \( y' \):
\[
y' = y (\ln(x) + 1)
\]
Since \( y = x^x \), substituting back gives:
\[
y' = x^x (\ln(x) + 1)
\]

Therefore, the derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( x^x (1 + \ln(x)) \).

To find the derivative of \( y = x^x \), we'll use logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Take the Natural Logarithm:**
   Let \( y = x^x \). Taking the natural logarithm of both sides gives:
   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
   \]
   
2. **Simplify Using Logarithm Properties:**
   Use the property \( \ln(a^b) = b\ln(a) \):
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]

3. **Differentiate Both Sides with Respect to \( x \):**
   
   - **Left Side:** Differentiate \( \ln(y) \) using the chain rule:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot y'
     \]
     
   - **Right Side:** Differentiate \( x \cdot \ln(x) \) using the product rule:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[x \cdot \ln(x)] = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1
     \]
     
4. **Set the Derivatives Equal and Solve for \( y' \):**
   \[
   \frac{y'}{y} = \ln(x) + 1
   \]
   Multiply both sides by \( y \):
   \[
   y' = y (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

5. **Substitute Back \( y = x^x \):**
   \[
   y' = x^x (1 + \ln(x))
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
(f \cdot g)' = f' \cdot g + f \cdot g'

- Let \( f(x) = x \)
- Let \( g(x) = e^x \)

\[
\frac{d}{dx} [x \cdot e^x] = f'(x) \cdot g(x) + f(x) \cdot g'(x)
\]
Substitute the derivatives we found:
\[
= 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
\]

f'(x) = u'*v + u*v' = 2x * e^x + x² * e^x.

- The derivative of u = e^x remains e^x, so u' = e^x.
- The derivative of v = 2x + x² is straightforward: v' = 2 + 2x.

Applying the product rule:

f''(x) = u'*v + u*v' = e^x*(2x + x²) + e^x*(2 + 2x).

- The terms with x: 2x and another 2x, which adds up to 4x.
- The constant term: 2.

So putting it all together inside the brackets gives us x² + 4x + 2.

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we use the quotient rule or recognize it as a composite function for the chain rule.

**Using Quotient Rule:**
- Let numerator \( u = 1 \), denominator \( v = x^2 + 1 \).
- \( u' = 0 \) and \( v' = 2x \).
- Applying the quotient rule: 
  \[
  f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{0 \cdot (x^2 + 1) - 1 \cdot 2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} = \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
  \]

**Using Chain Rule:**
- Rewrite \( f(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1} \).
- Let \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \), so \( f(g(x)) = g^{-1}(x) \).
- Derivatives:
  - \( f'(g(x)) = -g(x)^{-2} \)
  - \( g'(x) = 2x \)
- Applying chain rule: 
  \[
  f'(x) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) = -g(x)^{-2} \cdot 2x = \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
  \]

Both methods yield the same result.

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \) is \( f'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \).

The derivative of f(x) = \(\frac{\ln x}{x^2}\) can be found using the quotient rule. 

Let:
- \( u = \ln x \) ⇒ \( du/dx = \frac{1}{x} \)
- \( v = x^2 \) ⇒ \( dv/dx = 2x \)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{v \cdot du - u \cdot dv}{v^2} = \frac{x^2 \cdot \frac{1}{x} - \ln x \cdot 2x}{(x^2)^2}
\]
Simplifying:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{x - 2x \ln x}{x^4} = \frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3}
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative is \(\frac{1 - 2\ln x}{x^3}\).

To find the second derivative of \( f(x) = x^3 \ln x \), we'll proceed step by step.

**Step 1: Find the First Derivative**

We start by finding the first derivative \( f'(x) \).

Given:
\[ f(x) = x^3 \ln x \]

Using the **Product Rule** for differentiation, which states that if \( f(x) = u(x)v(x) \), then:
\[ f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \]

Let:
- \( u(x) = x^3 \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = 3x^2 \\
v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
f'(x) = 3x^2 \cdot \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} \\
= 3x^2 \ln x + x^2
\]

**Step 2: Find the Second Derivative**

Next, we find the second derivative \( f''(x) \) by differentiating \( f'(x) \).

Given:
\[ f'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2 \]

We'll differentiate each term separately.

1. **Differentiate \( 3x^2 \ln x \):**

   Again, use the product rule with:
   - \( u(x) = 3x^2 \)
   - \( v(x) = \ln x \)

   Find their derivatives:
   \[
   u'(x) = 6x \\
   v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
   \]

   Apply the product rule:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} [3x^2 \ln x] = 6x \cdot \ln x + 3x^2 \cdot \frac{1}{x} \\
   = 6x \ln x + 3x
   \]

2. **Differentiate \( x^2 \):**

\[
\frac{d}{dx} [x^2] = 2x
\]

**Combine the results:**
\[
f''(x) = (6x \ln x + 3x) + 2x \\
= 6x \ln x + 5x
\]

We can factor out \( x \) for simplicity:
\[
f''(x) = x(6 \ln x + 5)
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{ f''(x) = x (6 \ln x + 5) }
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u}{v} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
\left( \frac{u}{v} \right)' = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
\]

Here, let:
- \( u = e^x \)
- \( v = 1 + e^x \)

\[
u' = \frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = e^x
\]
\[
v' = \frac{d}{dx} (1 + e^x) = e^x
\]

\[

\]

e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x) = e^x + (e^x)^2 - (e^x)^2 = e^x
\]

- \( h'(x) = 2x \)

\[

To find the derivative of \( \sec x \) with respect to \( x \), we'll use the quotient rule since \( \sec x = \frac{1}{\cos x} \).

**Step 1: Apply the Quotient Rule**

The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u}{v} \), its derivative is:
\[
\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{u}{v}\right) = \frac{v \cdot u' - u \cdot v'}{v^2}
\]
Here, let \( u = 1 \) and \( v = \cos x \).

**Step 2: Compute the Derivatives**

- The derivative of \( u = 1 \) with respect to \( x \) is:
  \[
  u' = 0
  \]
  
- The derivative of \( v = \cos x \) with respect to \( x \) is:
  \[
  v' = -\sin x
  \]

**Step 3: Substitute into the Quotient Rule Formula**

Substituting \( u \), \( u' \), \( v \), and \( v' \) into the quotient rule formula:
\[
\frac{d}{dx}\left(\sec x\right) = \frac{\cos x \cdot 0 - 1 \cdot (-\sin x)}{\cos^2 x} = \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Expression**

Notice that:
\[
\frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x} = \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} = \tan x \cdot \sec x
\]
Therefore,
\[
\frac{d}{dx}\left(\sec x\right) = \sec x \tan x
\]

**Final Answer:**

The limit of sin(x)/x as x approaches 0 is a well-known standard limit in calculus, equal to 1. This can be shown using various methods such as Taylor series expansion, L'Hospital's Rule, or geometric intuition.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Recognize the Indeterminate Form:** Plugging x = 0 into sin(x)/x gives 0/0, which is an indeterminate form.
2. **Apply Standard Limit Result:** The standard limit lim_{x→0} (sin x)/x equals 1.
3. **Verification via Taylor Series:**
   - Taylor series expansion of sin(x) around 0: sin(x) = x - x³/6 + x⁵/120 - ...
   - Dividing by x: (sin x)/x = 1 - x²/6 + x⁴/120 - ...
   - As x approaches 0, higher power terms vanish, leaving the limit as 1.
4. **Alternative Approach using L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Differentiate numerator and denominator:
     - d/dx (sin x) = cos x
     - d/dx (x) = 1
   - New limit becomes lim_{x→0} cos x / 1 = cos(0) = 1.

**Final Answer:** The limit is 1.

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \), we can use several methods to simplify and evaluate the expression.

First, we factor out \( x \) from inside the square root:

\[
\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = \sqrt{x^2 \left(1 + \frac{2}{x}\right)} = x \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
\]

Next, we approximate the square root term for large \( x \):

\[
\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} \approx 1 + \frac{1}{x}
\]

Subtracting \( x \) from this approximation:

\[
x \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - x \approx x \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right) - x = x + 1 - x = 1
\]

To verify, we can use the binomial expansion for \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} \):

\[
\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} \approx x + 1 - \frac{1}{2(x+1)}
\]

Subtracting \( x \) gives:

\[
\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \approx 1 - \frac{1}{2(x+1)}
\]

As \( x \) approaches infinity, the term \( \frac{1}{2(x+1)} \) approaches zero, confirming that the limit is 1.

Thus, the limit is:

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 2}} \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Factor the Numerator:**
   
   The numerator \( x^2 - 4 \) is a difference of squares and can be factored as:
   \[
   x^2 - 4 = (x + 2)(x - 2)
   \]
   
2. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression:
   \[
   \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2} = \frac{(x + 2)(x - 2)}{x - 2}
   \]
   
   Since \( x \neq 2 \), we can cancel out the \( (x - 2) \) terms:
   \[
   \frac{(x + 2)(x - 2)}{x - 2} = x + 2
   \]
   
3. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   
   Now, take the limit as \( x \) approaches 2:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 2}} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4
   \]

2. **Apply the Leading Coefficient Rule:**
   When both polynomials have the same highest degree, the limit as \(x\) approaches infinity is equal to the ratio of their leading coefficients.

   - Leading coefficient of the denominator: \(7\)

The limit evaluates to 1 because as \( x \) approaches zero from the positive side, \( x^x \) approaches 1. This can be shown using logarithmic transformations and L'Hospital's Rule. 

Step-by-step explanation:

1. Rewrite \( x^x \) as \( e^{x \ln(x)} \).
2. Compute the limit of the exponent: \( \lim_{x \to 0^+} x \ln(x) \).
3. Rewrite this as \( \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x} \), which is an indeterminate form \( -\infty / \infty \).
4. Apply L'Hospital's Rule: differentiate numerator and denominator.
5. Derivatives give \( \frac{1/x}{-1/x^2} = -x \).
6. Compute the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 from the right, which is 0.
7. Thus, \( e^{\text{exponent}} = e^0 = 1 \).

Now evaluating that simpler limit: as x approaches infinity, 1/x gets closer and closer to zero because dividing 1 by a very large number gives a very small result approaching zero. Therefore, the original limit should also approach zero since applying L'Hospital's Rule gave us this equivalent expression whose limit is known.

I don't think I need to do anything else here. Since we've simplified it using L'Hospital's and found that the limit is zero, there shouldn't be any other steps required. Just making sure I didn't confuse the rates of growth: logarithmic functions grow much slower than linear ones. So yeah, dividing a slow-growing function by a fast-growing one would indeed approach zero.

I don't think there are any alternative methods here because L'Hospital's is straightforward in this case and confirms that the limit is zero.

The limit as x approaches infinity of (ln x)/x can be evaluated using L'Hospital's Rule since both the numerator and denominator approach infinity. Applying L'Hospital's Rule:

lim_{x→∞} (d/dx ln x) / (d/dx x) = lim_{x→∞} (1/x)/1 = lim_{x→∞} 1/x = 0.

Therefore, the limit is zero because as x grows without bound, 1/x approaches zero.

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will use basic differentiation rules.

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \) with respect to \( x \) is:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 6x
     \]
   
   - The derivative of \( -5x \) with respect to \( x \) is:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
   
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \) is:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives:**
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{f'(x)=6x-5}

lim(h→0) [e^(x+h) - e^x] / h

Simplify that expression inside the limit: e^(x+h) is e^x * e^h because of exponent rules (a^{b+c} = a^b * a^c). So,

[e^x * e^h - e^x] / h = e^x [e^h - 1] / h

So the limit becomes:

lim(h→0) e^x * [e^h - 1] / h

Since e^x doesn't depend on h, it can be pulled out of the limit:

e^x * lim(h→0) [e^h - 1]/h

Now, I remember that the limit as h approaches 0 of (e^h - 1)/h is equal to 1. Let me see if that makes sense.

If h is very small, e^h can be approximated by its Taylor series expansion: e^h = 1 + h + h²/2! + ... So,

(e^h - 1)/h ≈ (1 + h + h²/2! - 1)/h = (h + h²/2!)/h = 1 + h/2!

As h approaches 0, the term h/2! goes to 0, so the limit is indeed 1.

Therefore,

lim(h→0) [e^h - 1]/h = 1

So putting it all together:

d/dx (e^x) = e^x * 1 = e^x

Okay, that makes sense. So the derivative of e^x with respect to x is just e^x itself.

I think I've got it right. It's because e has a unique property where its rate of change is proportional to its current value. This is why exponential functions are so important in modeling growth and decay processes, like population growth or radioactive decay.

The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is found using the definition of the derivative and properties of the exponential function.

Step-by-step explanation:
1. Start with \( f(x) = e^x \).
2. Apply the definition of the derivative: \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^{x+h} - e^x}{h} \).
3. Simplify using exponent rules: \( e^{x+h} = e^x \cdot e^h \), leading to \( \frac{e^x (e^h - 1)}{h} \).
4. Factor out \( e^x \) since it's constant with respect to \( h \): \( e^x \cdot \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h} \).
5. Recognize that the limit \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h} = 1 \).
6. Therefore, the derivative is \( e^x \).

= [cos²x + sin²x] / cos²x
= 1 / cos²x
= sec²x.

The derivative of f(x) = tan x can be found using the quotient rule. Since tan x is equal to sin x over cos x, we apply the rule as follows:

Let g(x) = sin x and h(x) = cos x. The derivatives are g'(x) = cos x and h'(x) = -sin x.

Applying the quotient rule:
f'(x) = [g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)] / [h(x)]²
= [cos x * cos x - sin x * (-sin x)] / (cos x)^2
= [cos²x + sin²x] / cos²x

Using the Pythagorean identity, this simplifies to 1 / cos²x, which is equal to sec²x.

**Answer:** The derivative of tan x is sec²x.

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = x \cdot e^x \), we'll use the **product rule** from calculus. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is:

\[ h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x) \]

Let's identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):

- Let \( u(x) = x \)
- Then, \( u'(x) = 1 \)

- Let \( v(x) = e^x \)
- The derivative of \( v(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is also \( e^x \), so \( v'(x) = e^x \)

Now, applying the product rule:

\[
\begin{align*}
f'(x) &= u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x) \\
&= 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x \\
&= e^x + x \cdot e^x
\end{align*}
\]

We can factor out \( e^x \):

\[
f'(x) = (1 + x)e^x
\]

**Final Answer:**

\[ f'(x) = (1 + x)e^x \]

**Step 1: Find the First Derivative (\( f'(x) \))**

We start with:
\[
f(x) = x^2 e^x
\]

Apply the **Product Rule**, which states that if \( h(x) = u(x)v(x) \), then \( h'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \).

Let:
- \( u(x) = x^2 \)
- \( v(x) = e^x \)

Compute the derivatives:
\[
u'(x) = 2x
\]
\[
v'(x) = e^x
\]

Apply the Product Rule:
\[
f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

f'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x

- Let \( u(x) = 2x \)
- Let \( v(x) = e^x \)

u'(x) = 2

v'(x) = e^x
\]

- Let \( u(x) = x^2 \)

u'(x) = 2x

The derivative of f(x) = \(\frac{1}{x^2 + 1}\) can be found using the chain rule or the quotient rule. Applying either method yields the same result: \(f'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express f(x) as a composite function:**
   Let \(u(w) = \frac{1}{w} = w^{-1}\), where \(w = v(x) = x^2 + 1\).

2. **Differentiate the outer function with respect to w:**
   \(\frac{du}{dw} = -w^{-2}\).

3. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to x:**
   \(\frac{dv}{dx} = 2x\).

4. **Apply the chain rule:**
   \(f'(x) = \frac{du}{dw} \times \frac{dw}{dx} = -w^{-2} \times 2x\).

5. **Substitute back w = x² + 1:**
   \(f'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}\).

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of f(x) is \(-\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}\).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we'll use the **chain rule**. The chain rule states that if you have a composite function \( h(x) = g(f(x)) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is given by:

\[
h'(x) = g'(f(x)) \cdot f'(x)
\]

In our case:
- The **outer function** \( g(u) = \ln(u) \), so its derivative is \( g'(u) = \frac{1}{u} \).
- The **inner function** \( f(x) = \sin x \), whose derivative is \( f'(x) = \cos x \).

Applying the chain rule:

\[
f'(x) = g'(\sin x) \cdot f'(x) = \frac{1}{\sin x} \cdot \cos x
\]

Simplifying further:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{\cos x}{\sin x} = \cot x
\]

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{\cot x}

To find the second derivative of \( f(x) = x^3 \ln x \), we'll proceed step-by-step.

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative
We start by finding the first derivative \( f'(x) \).

Given:
\[ f(x) = x^3 \ln x \]

We can use the **product rule** for differentiation, which states that if \( f(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then:
\[ f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x) \]

Let:
- \( u(x) = x^3 \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[ u'(x) = 3x^2 \]
\[ v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
f'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x}
\]

Simplify \( x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} \):
\[
f'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative
Now, we'll find the second derivative by differentiating \( f'(x) \).

Given:
\[ f'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2 \]

We'll differentiate each term separately.

#### Differentiating \( 3x^2 \ln x \):
Again, use the **product rule** for this part.
\[
u(x) = 3x^2 \quad \Rightarrow \quad u'(x) = 6x
\]
\[
v(x) = \ln x \quad \Rightarrow \quad v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

Apply the product rule:
\[
\frac{d}{dx} (3x^2 \ln x) = 6x \ln x + 3x^2 \cdot \frac{1}{x}
\]
Simplify \( 3x^2 \cdot \frac{1}{x} \):
\[
= 6x \ln x + 3x
\]

#### Differentiating \( x^2 \):
\[
\frac{d}{dx} (x^2) = 2x
\]

Now, combine both derivatives:
\[
f''(x) = (6x \ln x + 3x) + 2x
\]
Simplify by combining like terms:
\[
f''(x) = 6x \ln x + 5x
\]

### Final Answer
The second derivative of \( f(x) = x^3 \ln x \) is:
\[ \boxed{6x \ln x + 5x} \]

First, I need to find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \).

I recognize that this is a quotient of two functions. To differentiate it, I'll use the Quotient Rule, which states that if you have a function \( \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is \( \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} \).

Let me identify \( u(x) = e^x \) and \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \).

Next, I'll find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
- The derivative of \( u(x) = e^x \) is \( u'(x) = e^x \).
- The derivative of \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \) is \( v'(x) = e^x \).

Now, I'll apply the Quotient Rule:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Simplifying the numerator:
\[
e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x) = e^x + (e^x)^2 - (e^x)^2 = e^x
\]

So, the derivative simplifies to:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]
</think>

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we'll use the **Quotient Rule**. The Quotient Rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), then its derivative is given by:

\[
h'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

Let's identify the numerator and denominator of our function:
- \( u(x) = e^x \)
- \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \)

Now, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
- \( u'(x) = e^x \) (since the derivative of \( e^x \) is itself)
- \( v'(x) = e^x \) (again, the derivative of \( e^x \) is itself)

Applying the Quotient Rule:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator:

\[
e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x) = e^x + (e^x)^2 - (e^x)^2 = e^x
\]

So, the derivative simplifies to:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{e^{x}}{(1 + e^{x})^{2}}}
\]

To find the derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\), we start by setting \(y = \tan^{-1} x\). This implies that \(\tan y = x\).

Differentiating both sides with respect to \(x\):
- The left side, using the chain rule, becomes \(\sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}\).
- The right side differentiates to 1.

Thus, we have:
\[
\sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
\]

Solving for \(\frac{dy}{dx}\):
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2 y}
\]

Since \(y = \tan^{-1} x\), we use the identity \(\sec^2 y = 1 + \tan^2 y = 1 + x^2\) to substitute back:
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
\]

**Answer:**  
The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) is \(\boxed{\dfrac{1}{1+x^{2}}}\).

The product rule states that if \( f(x) = u(x)v(x) \), then \( f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \).

Next, I'll compute the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \). The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) is \( u'(x) = 1 \), and the derivative of \( v(x) = \ln x \) is \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

\[
f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \frac{1}{x}
\]
Simplifying this expression gives:
\[
f'(x) = \ln x + 1
\]

Therefore, the derivative of \( f(x) = x \ln x \) is \( f'(x) = \ln x + 1 \).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = u(x)v(x) \), its derivative is given by:

\[
f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

In this case, let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = 1
\]
\[
v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]
\[
f'(x) = (1)(\ln x) + (x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
\]
\[
f'(x) = \ln x + 1
\]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\ln x + 1}

The derivative of \(\frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\) is found using the quotient rule, resulting in:

\[
\frac{(x^2 + 1)\cos x - 2x \sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Identify \(u\) and \(v\)**:
   - Let \(u = \sin x\) (numerator).
   - Let \(v = x^2 + 1\) (denominator).

2. **Find derivatives \(u'\) and \(v'\)**:
   - \(u' = \cos x\).
   - \(v' = 2x\).

3. **Apply the quotient rule**:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{v \cdot u' - u \cdot v'}{v^2} = \frac{(x^2 + 1)\cos x - \sin x \cdot 2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify if possible**:
   - The expression is already simplified.

Final Answer: {d/dx [sin x/(x² + 1)] = [(x² + 1)cos x - 2x sin x]/(x² + 1)²}

First off, the first derivative. Since f(x) is an exponential function with a composite exponent (x²), I should use the chain rule here. The chain rule says that if you have e^u, where u is a function of x, then the derivative is e^u times the derivative of u.

So, let me set u = x². Then f(x) = e^{u}, so the first derivative f'(x) should be e^{x²} multiplied by the derivative of x². The derivative of x squared is 2x, right? So putting it all together, f'(x) = 2x * e^{x²}. That seems straightforward.

Now, onto the second derivative. This means I need to differentiate f'(x), which is 2x times e^{x²} again. This looks like a product of two functions: one is 2x (a simple polynomial) and the other is e^{x²} (which we already know how to handle). So, for differentiating a product, I should use the product rule.

The product rule states that if you have two functions multiplied together, say g(x) and h(x), then their derivative is g'(x)h(x) + g(x)h'(x). Applying this here:

Let me denote g(x) = 2x and h(x) = e^{x²}.

f''(x) = g'(x)h(x) + g(x)h'(x)
= 2 * e^{x²} + (2x) * (2x * e^{x²})

The second derivative of f(x) = e^{x²} is calculated by first finding the first derivative using the chain rule, resulting in f'(x) = 2x e^{x²}. Then applying the product rule to differentiate f'(x), we get:

f''(x) = d/dx [2x] * e^{x²} + 2x * d/dx [e^{x²}]  
= 2 e^{x²} + 2x * (2x e^{x²})  
= 2 e^{x²} + 4x² e^{x²}  
= e^{x²}(2 + 4x²)

So, the second derivative is f''(x) = e^{x²}(4x² + 2).

Here, f is 1 and g is cos(x). The derivatives are f' = 0 and g' = -sin(x).

To find the derivative of \(\sec x\), we'll use basic differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express \(\sec x\) in terms of cosine:**
   \[
   \sec x = \frac{1}{\cos x}
   \]

2. **Apply the quotient rule for differentiation:**
   The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)} \), its derivative is:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2}
   \]
   
3. **Identify \(g(x)\) and \(h(x)\):**
   Here, \( g(x) = 1 \) and \( h(x) = \cos x \).

4. **Compute the derivatives of \(g(x)\) and \(h(x)\):**
   \[
   g'(x) = 0
   \]
   \[
   h'(x) = -\sin x
   \]

5. **Plug these into the quotient rule formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{0 \cdot \cos x - 1 \cdot (-\sin x)}{\cos^2 x} = \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x}
   \]

6. **Simplify using trigonometric identities:**
   Recognize that:
   \[
   \sec x = \frac{1}{\cos x}, \quad \tan x = \frac{\sin x}{\cos x}
   \]
   
7. **Express the derivative in terms of sec and tan:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \sec x \cdot \tan x
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \(\sec x\) with respect to \(x\) is:

\[
\boxed{\sec(x)\,\tan(x)}
\]
